I work as a cashier, and lately I keep getting stuck doing things at the register that just... don't really fit the moment. \n\nLike, sometimes I'll start tapping the scanner in a specific pattern instead of ringing things up, or I'll just completely freeze with my hand hovering over the open cash drawer while a customer is standing right there waiting for their change. It's been happening enough that it actually got me written up by my manager last week, so... I figured I needed to come in and figure out what's going on.\n\nDoctor: When you tap the scanner in that specific pattern, do you feel like you have to do it a certain way, or does it just happen automatically without you really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I don't think I feel like I have to do it... like, there's no specific reason or urge in my head. It just sort of happens. \n\nI'll be standing there, and then I just realize my hand is already doing it. I don't really think about it before it starts, it just kind of... happens on its own.\n\nDoctor: If you tried to stop yourself from doing that tapping pattern, would you feel anxious or uncomfortable, or would it feel fine to just skip it?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'd feel anxious. It's not like I'm doing it to relieve stress or anything like that. \n\nIf I catch myself doing it and make myself stop, or if a customer says something and interrupts me, it feels completely fine. I don't get uncomfortable or upset about not finishing the pattern. It's really just... well, the hard part is catching myself in the first place, since my hand just sort of goes on autopilot. But stopping it doesn't bother me emotionally at all.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing the tapping pattern or freezing at the cash drawer, do you have any thoughts or mental images that you feel like you need to do it a certain way, or is it purely a physical movement without any thoughts attached to it?\n\nPatient: No, there's no thoughts or pictures in my head like that. I don't have a specific way it needs to be done. It's just... it's just my hand moving, really. There's nothing going on in my mind when it happens, no thoughts attached to it at all. It's just purely physical.\n\nDoctor: When you do the tapping pattern at the register, does it look like the same exact movements each time, or does it vary?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much the same pattern every time. That's actually why I came in, to be honest. At the register, I just keep getting stuck doing these things that really don't fit the moment. Like tapping the scanner in that exact pattern, or sometimes I just freeze with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer... and the customer is just standing there waiting for their change. It's becoming a real problem. Last week, it actually got me written up at work.\n\nDoctor: Before your hand starts tapping or before you freeze, do you ever feel a buildup of tension, an urge, or a sensation somewhere in your body that makes you feel like you need to move?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't feel anything like that. There's no buildup of tension or any kind of urge. I don't feel a sensation in my hand or anywhere else before it happens. It just sort of... starts on its own, really, without any warning at all.\n\nDoctor: When you tap the scanner in that pattern, is it always the exact same number of taps and in the exact same sequence, or does it sometimes vary a bit?\n\nPatient: It's always exactly the same. Like I was saying before, that's why I'm here... I just get stuck doing these things that make no sense for what I'm doing. It's the exact same tapping pattern on the scanner, or I just completely freeze with my hand hovering over the cash drawer. The customer is just standing there waiting for me to hand them their change, and I'm just stuck. It's exactly the same every time, and like I said, it got me written up last week.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I work as a cashier, and lately I keep getting stuck doing things at the register that just... don't really fit the moment. \n\nLike, sometimes I'll start tapping the scanner in a specific pattern instead of ringing things up, or I'll just completely freeze with my hand hovering over the open cash drawer while a customer is standing right there waiting for their change. I figured I needed to come in and figure out what's going on.\n\nDoctor: When you tap the scanner in that specific pattern, do you feel like you have to do it a certain way, or does it just happen automatically without you really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I don't think I feel like I have to do it... like, there's no specific reason or urge in my head. It just sort of happens. \n\nI'll be standing there, and then I just realize my hand is already doing it. I don't really think about it before it starts, it just kind of... happens on its own.\n\nDoctor: If you tried to stop yourself from doing that tapping pattern, would you feel anxious or uncomfortable, or would it feel fine to just skip it?\n\nPatient: I don't think I'd feel anxious. It's not like I'm doing it to relieve stress or anything like that. \n\nIf I catch myself doing it and make myself stop, or if a customer says something and interrupts me, it feels completely fine. I don't get uncomfortable or upset about not finishing the pattern. It's really just... well, the hard part is catching myself in the first place, since my hand just sort of goes on autopilot. But stopping it doesn't bother me emotionally at all.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing the tapping pattern or freezing at the cash drawer, do you have any thoughts or mental images that you feel like you need to do it a certain way, or is it purely a physical movement without any thoughts attached to it?\n\nPatient: No, there's no thoughts or pictures in my head like that. I don't have a specific way it needs to be done. It's just... it's just my hand moving, really. There's nothing going on in my mind when it happens, no thoughts attached to it at all. It's just purely physical.\n\nDoctor: When you do the tapping pattern at the register, does it look like the same exact movements each time, or does it vary?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much the same pattern every time. There's no tingling or... or tightness or anything before it starts. I'm just ringing up groceries, and then suddenly I'm tapping the scanner or my hand is just frozen over the drawer. It's just purely physical.\n\nDoctor: When you do the tapping pattern at the register, does it look like the same exact movements each time, or does it vary?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much the same pattern every time. That's actually why I came in, to be honest. At the register, I just keep getting stuck doing these things that really don't fit the moment. Like tapping the scanner in that exact pattern, or sometimes I just freeze with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer... and the customer is just standing there waiting for their change. It's becoming a real problem. Last week, it actually got me written up at work.\n\nDoctor: Before your hand starts tapping or before you freeze, do you ever feel a buildup of tension, an urge, or a sensation somewhere in your body that makes you feel like you need to move?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't feel anything like that. There's no buildup of tension or any kind of urge. I don't feel a sensation in my hand or anywhere else before it happens. It just sort of... starts on its own, really, without any warning at all.\n\nDoctor: When you tap the scanner in that pattern, is it always the exact same number of taps and in the exact same sequence, or does it sometimes vary a bit?\n\nPatient: It's always exactly the same. Like I was saying before, that's why I'm here... I just get stuck doing these things that make no sense for what I'm doing. It's the exact same tapping pattern on the scanner, or I just completely freeze with my hand hovering over the cash drawer. The customer is just standing there waiting for me to hand them their change, and I'm just stuck. It's exactly the same every time, and like I said, it got me written up last week.\n\nDoctor: If you wanted to, could you deliberately do that tapping pattern on the scanner right now, or does it only happen on its own when you're not paying attention?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I've never actually tried to do it on purpose. It just... it just happens on its own when I'm standing there working. I don't know if I could just sit here and recreate the exact thing for you right now.\n\nDoctor: I'd like to try something. Can you show me right now how you tap the scanner? Try to do that tapping pattern for me, just here in my office.\n\nPatient: I can try, I guess. Um... let me think about how it goes. It's usually... tap, tap... pause... tap. Something like that. But doing it here, on your desk... it doesn't feel the same. It feels forced. When I'm at work, it just happens to me. I don't know if I'm doing it exactly right right now because I'm actually thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work and you notice yourself starting to do that tapping pattern, if you really focus your attention on your hand and try hard to do it differently—like tapping in a different sequence or a different number of times—can you actually change what your hand is doing, or does it keep doing the same pattern no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: I can't change it. That's... that's the whole reason I came in. Like I was saying, I keep getting stuck doing these things at the register that just don't fit what's happening in the moment. I try to stop or do it differently, but I can't. I just keep tapping the scanner in that same pattern, or I completely freeze with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is just standing there waiting. I can't control it, no matter how hard I try, and... well, like I mentioned, it got me written up last week.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work and you catch yourself starting to tap or freeze, is there ever even a tiny sensation—like a tingling, tightness, or restless feeling—anywhere in your body right before it happens, or does it truly come out of nowhere with absolutely no warning sensation at all?\n\nPatient: I haven't noticed anything like that. There's no tingling or... or tightness or anything before it starts. I'm just ringing up groceries, and then suddenly I'm tapping the scanner or my hand is just frozen over the drawer. It really does just come out of nowhere, without any kind of physical warning that I can feel.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this tapping pattern and freezing at work? Did it start suddenly one day, or did it gradually develop over time?\n\nPatient: It's been about three months. The voices and... well, these more clearly out-of-place moments, like the tapping and the freezing at the register, have been happening for about the last three months. I wouldn't say it just happened overnight suddenly, but looking back, three months ago is when it all really started to become an issue.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned 'voices' just now. Can you tell me more about that? Are you hearing voices that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I am hearing voices. Most days, actually. It usually sounds like a couple of people talking just off to the side of me. Sometimes they call my name, like it's coming from the stockroom, or they'll tell me I rang something up wrong. But... whenever I turn around to look, there's nobody there. And I know the customers or my coworkers don't hear it, because they never react. It's just me hearing them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

The customer is just standing there waiting for me to hand them their change, and I'm just stuck. It's exactly the same every time, and like I said, it got me written up last week.\n\nDoctor: If you wanted to, could you deliberately do that tapping pattern on the scanner right now, or does it only happen on its own when you're not paying attention?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I've never actually tried to do it on purpose. It just... it just happens on its own when I'm standing there working. I don't know if I could just sit here and recreate the exact thing for you right now.\n\nDoctor: I'd like to try something. Can you show me right now how you tap the scanner? Try to do that tapping pattern for me, just here in my office.\n\nPatient: I can try, I guess. Um... let me think about how it goes. It's usually... tap, tap... pause... tap. Something like that. But doing it here, on your desk... it doesn't feel the same. It feels forced. When I'm at work, it just happens to me. I don't know if I'm doing it exactly right right now because I'm actually thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work and you notice yourself starting to do that tapping pattern, if you really focus your attention on your hand and try hard to do it differently—like tapping in a different sequence or a different number of times—can you actually change what your hand is doing, or does it keep doing the same pattern no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: I can't change it. That's... that's the whole reason I came in. Like I was saying, I keep getting stuck doing these things at the register that just don't fit what's happening in the moment. I try to stop or do it differently, but I can't. I just keep tapping the scanner in that same pattern, or I completely freeze with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is just standing there waiting. I can't control it, no matter how hard I try, and... well, like I mentioned, it got me written up last week.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work and you catch yourself starting to tap or freeze, is there ever even a tiny sensation—like a tingling, tightness, or restless feeling—anywhere in your body right before it happens, or does it truly come out of nowhere with absolutely no warning sensation at all?\n\nPatient: I haven't noticed anything like that. There's no tingling or... or tightness or anything before it starts. I'm just ringing up groceries, and then suddenly I'm tapping the scanner or my hand is just frozen over the drawer. It really does just come out of nowhere, without any kind of physical warning that I can feel.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this tapping pattern and freezing at work? Did it start suddenly one day, or did it gradually develop over time?\n\nPatient: It's been about three months. The voices and... well, these more clearly out-of-place moments, like the tapping and the freezing at the register, have been happening for about the last three months. I wouldn't say it just happened overnight suddenly, but looking back, three months ago is when it all really started to become an issue.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned 'voices' just now. Can you tell me more about that? Are you hearing voices that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I am hearing voices. Most days, actually. It usually sounds like a couple of people talking just off to the side of me. Sometimes they call my name, like it's coming from the stockroom, or they'll tell me I rang something up wrong. But... whenever I turn around to look, there's nobody there. And I know the customers or my coworkers don't hear it, because they never react. It's just me hearing them.\n\nDoctor: When you hear these voices calling your name or telling you that you rang something up wrong, do they sound like they're coming from outside your head—like actual people speaking around you—or do they sound like they're coming from inside your mind, like your own thoughts but in a voice?\n\nPatient: They definitely sound like they're coming from outside my head. It's not like a thought or an inner voice... it's like actual people speaking. Like I said, it sounds like they're just off to the side of me, or coming from the stockroom. It's loud enough and clear enough that I actually turn my head to look for who is talking, but... nobody is ever there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Try to do that tapping pattern for me, just here in my office.\n\nPatient: I can try, I guess. Um... let me think about how it goes. It's usually... tap, tap... pause... tap. Something like that. But doing it here, on your desk... it doesn't feel the same. It feels forced. When I'm at work, it just happens to me. I don't know if I'm doing it exactly right right now because I'm actually thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work and you notice yourself starting to do that tapping pattern, if you really focus your attention on your hand and try hard to do it differently—like tapping in a different sequence or a different number of times—can you actually change what your hand is doing, or does it keep doing the same pattern no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: I can't change it. That's... that's the whole reason I came in. Like I was saying, I keep getting stuck doing these things at the register that just don't fit what's happening in the moment. I try to stop or do it differently, but I can't. I just keep tapping the scanner in that same pattern, or I completely freeze with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is just standing there waiting. I can't control it, no matter how hard I try, and... well, like I mentioned, it got me written up last week.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work and you catch yourself starting to tap or freeze, is there ever even a tiny sensation—like a tingling, tightness, or restless feeling—anywhere in your body right before it happens, or does it truly come out of nowhere with absolutely no warning sensation at all?\n\nPatient: I haven't noticed anything like that. There's no tingling or... or tightness or anything before it starts. I'm just ringing up groceries, and then suddenly I'm tapping the scanner or my hand is just frozen over the drawer. It really does just come out of nowhere, without any kind of physical warning that I can feel.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this tapping pattern and freezing at work? Did it start suddenly one day, or did it gradually develop over time?\n\nPatient: It's been about three months. The voices and... well, these more clearly out-of-place moments, like the tapping and the freezing at the register, have been happening for about the last three months. I wouldn't say it just happened overnight suddenly, but looking back, three months ago is when it all really started to become an issue.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned 'voices' just now. Can you tell me more about that? Are you hearing voices that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I am hearing voices. Most days, actually. It usually sounds like a couple of people talking just off to the side of me. Sometimes they call my name, like it's coming from the stockroom, or they'll tell me I rang something up wrong. But... whenever I turn around to look, there's nobody there. And I know the customers or my coworkers don't hear it, because they never react. It's just me hearing them.\n\nDoctor: When you hear these voices calling your name or telling you that you rang something up wrong, do they sound like they're coming from outside your head—like actual people speaking around you—or do they sound like they're coming from inside your mind, like your own thoughts but in a voice?\n\nPatient: They definitely sound like they're coming from outside my head. It's not like a thought or an inner voice... it's like actual people speaking. Like I said, it sounds like they're just off to the side of me, or coming from the stockroom. It's loud enough and clear enough that I actually turn my head to look for who is talking, but... nobody is ever there.\n\nDoctor: Over these past three months when you've been hearing the voices, have you been hearing them pretty much every single day without breaks, or have there been periods of days or weeks where the voices completely stopped and you didn't hear anything at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure. I mean, I haven't really kept a calendar or anything to track them. Like I said before, it feels like it's most days. There might be a day here or there where it's quiet, or maybe I just don't notice them as much, but... I really couldn't tell you if there were whole weeks where they completely stopped. I just haven't noticed a long break like that, to be honest.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

The customer is just standing there waiting for me to hand them their change, and I'm just stuck. It's exactly the same every time, and like I said, it got me written up last week.\n\nDoctor: If you wanted to, could you deliberately do that tapping pattern on the scanner right now, or does it only happen on its own when you're not paying attention?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I've never actually tried to do it on purpose. It just... it just happens on its own when I'm standing there working. I don't know if I could just sit here and recreate the exact thing for you right now.\n\nDoctor: I'd like to try something. Can you show me right now how you tap the scanner? Try to do that tapping pattern for me, just here in my office.\n\nPatient: I can try, I guess. Um... let me think about how it goes. It's usually... tap, tap... pause... tap. Something like that. But doing it here, on your desk... it doesn't feel the same. It feels forced. When I'm at work, it just happens to me. I don't know if I'm doing it exactly right right now because I'm actually thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work and you notice yourself starting to do that tapping pattern, if you really focus your attention on your hand and try hard to do it differently—like tapping in a different sequence or a different number of times—can you actually change what your hand is doing, or does it keep doing the same pattern no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: I can't change it. That's... that's the whole reason I came in. Like I was saying, I keep getting stuck doing these things at the register that just don't fit what's happening in the moment. I try to stop or do it differently, but I can't. I just keep tapping the scanner in that same pattern, or I completely freeze with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is just standing there waiting. I can't control it, no matter how hard I try, and... well, like I mentioned, it got me written up last week.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work and you catch yourself starting to tap or freeze, is there ever even a tiny sensation—like a tingling, tightness, or restless feeling—anywhere in your body right before it happens, or does it truly come out of nowhere with absolutely no warning sensation at all?\n\nPatient: I haven't noticed anything like that. There's no tingling or... or tightness or anything before it starts. I'm just ringing up groceries, and then suddenly I'm tapping the scanner or my hand is just frozen over the drawer. It really does just come out of nowhere, without any kind of physical warning that I can feel.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this tapping pattern and freezing at work? Did it start suddenly one day, or did it gradually develop over time?\n\nPatient: It's been about three months. The voices and... well, these more clearly out-of-place moments, like the tapping and the freezing at the register, have been happening for about the last three months. I wouldn't say it just happened overnight suddenly, but looking back, three months ago is when it all really started to become an issue.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned 'voices' just now. Can you tell me more about that? Are you hearing voices that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I am hearing voices. Most days, actually. It usually sounds like a couple of people talking just off to the side of me. Sometimes they call my name, like it's coming from the stockroom, or they'll tell me I rang something up wrong. But... whenever I turn around to look, there's nobody there. And I know the customers or my coworkers don't hear it, because they never react. It's just me hearing them.\n\nDoctor: When you hear these voices calling your name or telling you that you rang something up wrong, do they sound like they're coming from outside your head—like actual people speaking around you—or do they sound like they're coming from inside your mind, like your own thoughts but in a voice?\n\nPatient: They definitely sound like they're coming from outside my head. It's not like a thought or an inner voice... it's like actual people speaking. Like I said, it sounds like they're just off to the side of me, or coming from the stockroom. It's loud enough and clear enough that I actually turn my head to look for who is talking, but... nobody is ever there.\n\nDoctor: Over these past three months when you've been hearing the voices, have you been hearing them pretty much every single day without breaks, or have there been periods of days or weeks where the voices completely stopped and you didn't hear anything at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure. I mean, I haven't really kept a calendar or anything to track them. Like I said before, it feels like it's most days. There might be a day here or there where it's quiet, or maybe I just don't notice them as much, but... I really couldn't tell you if there were whole weeks where they completely stopped. I just haven't noticed a long break like that, to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Since these voices started three months ago, have there been any stretches of time—say, a full month or more in a row—where you were hearing them almost every day without significant breaks, or has it been more scattered throughout the three months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it hasn't really been scattered. The voices... and just those other moments where things feel clearly out of place... they've been happening for about the last three months straight. So, yeah, I'd say it's definitely been a full month or more in a row of hearing them almost every day during this whole three-month stretch.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don't have a specific way it needs to be done. It's just... it's just my hand moving, really. There's nothing going on in my mind when it happens, no thoughts attached to it at all. It's just purely physical.\n\nDoctor: When you do the tapping pattern at the register, does it look like the same exact movements each time, or does it vary?\n\nPatient: It's pretty much the same pattern every time. That's actually why I came in, to be honest. At the register, I just keep getting stuck doing these things that really don't fit the moment. Like tapping the scanner in that exact pattern, or sometimes I just freeze with my hand hovering right over the open cash drawer... and the customer is just standing there waiting for their change. It's becoming a real problem. Last week, it actually got me written up at work.\n\nDoctor: Before your hand starts tapping or before you freeze, do you ever feel a buildup of tension, an urge, or a sensation somewhere in your body that makes you feel like you need to move?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't feel anything like that. There's no buildup of tension or any kind of urge. I don't feel a sensation in my hand or anywhere else before it happens. It just sort of... starts on its own, really, without any warning at all.\n\nDoctor: When you tap the scanner in that pattern, is it always the exact same number of taps and in the exact same sequence, or does it sometimes vary a bit?\n\nPatient: It's always exactly the same. Like I was saying before, that's why I'm here... I just get stuck doing these things that make no sense for what I'm doing. It's the exact same tapping pattern on the scanner, or I just completely freeze with my hand hovering over the cash drawer. The customer is just standing there waiting for me to hand them their change, and I'm just stuck. It's exactly the same every time, and like I said, it got me written up last week.\n\nDoctor: If you wanted to, could you deliberately do that tapping pattern on the scanner right now, or does it only happen on its own when you're not paying attention?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I've never actually tried to do it on purpose. It just... it just happens on its own when I'm standing there working. I don't know if I could just sit here and recreate the exact thing for you right now.\n\nDoctor: I'd like to try something. Can you show me right now how you tap the scanner? Try to do that tapping pattern for me, just here in my office.\n\nPatient: I can try, I guess. Um... let me think about how it goes. It's usually... tap, tap... pause... tap. Something like that. But doing it here, on your desk... it doesn't feel the same. It feels forced. When I'm at work, it just happens to me. I don't know if I'm doing it exactly right right now because I'm actually thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work and you notice yourself starting to do that tapping pattern, if you really focus your attention on your hand and try hard to do it differently—like tapping in a different sequence or a different number of times—can you actually change what your hand is doing, or does it keep doing the same pattern no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: I can't change it. That's... that's the whole reason I came in. Like I was saying, I keep getting stuck doing these things at the register that just don't fit what's happening in the moment. I try to stop or do it differently, but I can't. I just keep tapping the scanner in that same pattern, or I completely freeze with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is just standing there waiting. I can't control it, no matter how hard I try, and... well, like I mentioned, it got me written up last week.\n\nDoctor: When you're at work and you catch yourself starting to tap or freeze, is there ever even a tiny sensation—like a tingling, tightness, or restless feeling—anywhere in your body right before it happens, or does it truly come out of nowhere with absolutely no warning sensation at all?\n\nPatient: I haven't noticed anything like that. There's no tingling or... or tightness or anything before it starts. I'm just ringing up groceries, and then suddenly I'm tapping the scanner or my hand is just frozen over the drawer. It really does just come out of nowhere, without any kind of physical warning that I can feel.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this tapping pattern and freezing at work? The voices and... well, these more clearly out-of-place moments, like the tapping and the freezing at the register, have been happening for about the last three months. I wouldn't say it just happened overnight suddenly, but looking back, three months ago is when it all really started to become an issue.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned 'voices' just now. Can you tell me more about that? Are you hearing voices that other people can't hear?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I... I am hearing voices. Most days, actually. But... whenever I turn around to look, there's nobody there. And I know the customers or my coworkers don't hear it, because they never react. It's just me hearing them.\n\nDoctor: When you hear these voices calling your name or telling you that you rang something up wrong, do they sound like they're coming from outside your head—like actual people speaking around you—or do they sound like they're coming from inside your mind, like your own thoughts but in a voice?\n\nPatient: They definitely sound like they're coming from outside my head. It's not like a thought or an inner voice... it's like actual people speaking. Like I said, it sounds like they're just off to the side of me, or coming from the stockroom. It's loud enough and clear enough that I actually turn my head to look for who is talking, but... nobody is ever there.\n\nDoctor: Over these past three months when you've been hearing the voices, have you been hearing them pretty much every single day without breaks, or have there been periods of days or weeks where the voices completely stopped and you didn't hear anything at all?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure. I mean, I haven't really kept a calendar or anything to track them. Like I said before, it feels like it's most days. There might be a day here or there where it's quiet, or maybe I just don't notice them as much, but... I really couldn't tell you if there were whole weeks where they completely stopped.
